I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just focus on this one thing,” like finishing a report or checking an assay, something concrete where there’s an obvious next step. But it doesn’t stay there.\n\nOne worry kind of sets off another, and then another, and before I know it I’m not really doing the task anymore. I’m just sitting there staring at the screen, but in my head I’m running through every possible way things could go wrong. Like if this result is off, then maybe I missed something, and if I missed something then maybe the whole report is wrong, and then what does that mean for my work, or my future, or… it just branches out really fast.\n\nAnd I know from the outside it probably sounds like I should just be able to say, “Stop, go back to what you were doing,” but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple. It feels like if I don’t keep thinking about it, I’m being careless somehow. So no, I wouldn’t say I can control it most of the time. It feels more like it takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is worse than what most people would experience in the same situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is worse than what most people would have in the same situations. I know everyone worries about work or money or their future, so I don’t want to make it sound like I think I’m the only person who has stress. But the way it happens for me feels… disproportionate, I guess.\n\nLike, I came in because my mind is constantly jumping from one thing to the next. That’s the part that makes me feel like it’s more than normal worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it. Yeah… sometimes there’s a specific thing, but honestly it doesn’t take much. It can start with something concrete, like hearing about our lab’s new partnership, and then my brain immediately jumps to, “Okay, does that mean they’re restructuring, does that mean my position could get cut?” Even if nobody has said that. It just becomes this whole chain.

Or I’ll be working with data and notice one result that doesn’t look exactly how I expected, and then I’m thinking maybe my data are wrong, maybe I missed something obvious, maybe everyone’s going to realize I don’t know what I’m doing. And then somehow it doesn’t stay about the data. It turns into bills piling up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school… it’s like one small trigger opens up every other worry I have.

So yes, there are situations that set it off, especially anything around work or my future, but the problem is that once it starts, it spreads way beyond whatever actually happened.

I mean… I definitely get stuck thinking, and I try to “solve” the worry in my head, but I don’t think there’s a specific way I have to think about something to make the anxiety go down.\n\nLike, I’ll try to reason through it — almost like arguing with myself — “Okay, what evidence do I have that I messed this up?” or “What’s the realistic chance this actually happens?” But it doesn’t feel like a required mental ritual, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’m trying anything to get my brain to quiet down, and sometimes I end up going in circles.\n\nI don’t have a particular phrase I repeat or a certain mental order I have to follow. It’s just… a lot of analyzing, replaying, trying to reassure myself, and then the reassurance doesn’t really stick for long. So I guess the answer is no, not in a specific way that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d describe it as overwhelming in that way, no. I mean, I definitely get an urge to keep thinking about the worry, like to keep checking it from different angles in my head, but it doesn’t feel like, “I have to repeat this action” or “I have to do this mental step again or something terrible will happen.”\n\nIt’s more like… I feel uneasy, and then I want relief, so I might go back over something again mentally, or I might feel pulled to reassure myself somehow. But it’s not a specific repeated act, and it’s not like I have a set mental thing I repeat until it feels “right.” It’s just hard to stop the worrying once it gets going.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not really. I don’t notice a strong urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to the worries, at least not in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, although I don’t always call it “avoidance” in the moment. I’ll tell myself I’m just postponing something until I’m in a better headspace, or that I need more time to do it properly, but then it kind of turns into me not doing it.\n\nThe biggest example is at work. I missed an internal lab deadline because I just couldn’t get myself organized enough to finish the writing and review the data. It wasn’t that I didn’t care — honestly, I cared too much, if anything — but every time I sat down to go through it, I’d get tangled up in whether the wording was right, whether I’d missed something in the data, whether someone would question how I presented it. So I kept circling back, trying to make it feel safe to submit, and then I lost time. After that, even opening the report started to feel like this huge thing.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. Routine responsibilities feel like too much lately. Preparing reports, even ones that should be straightforward, feels like this mountain. Outside of work, I’ve skipped art nights I usually like going to because I start worrying I’ll be too tired, or awkward, or behind on other things, and then I just don’t go. Even cooking for my family has started to feel hard to keep up with, like there are too many steps and too many ways to mess it up or disappoint people.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do avoid things — or I delay them until they become worse. It’s frustrating because these are normal parts of my life, not huge dramatic things, but they feel like more than I can manage sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific thoughts or mental images that you feel compelled to think about to try to relieve your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t think there are specific thoughts or images that I feel compelled to bring up on purpose to calm myself down.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck on certain worries, and sometimes a particular scenario will keep replaying in my mind, but it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing a specific image or thought because it relieves the anxiety. If anything, it usually makes me more anxious. Like I’ll try to think through it because I’m hoping I can settle it somehow, but there isn’t some exact mental picture or phrase where I think, “Okay, if I think this, I’ll feel better.”\n\nIt’s more vague and messy than that. I’ll go back over the concern, try to remember details, try to imagine how something might turn out, try to reassure myself that it’s probably fine… but it’s not like a specific thought I have to think. It’s just my mind sort of grabbing onto the worry and not letting go.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not in that specific way. I haven’t really noticed anything like a required mental image or thought that I use to relieve the anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life, like work, or do they cover a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one area. Work is a big one, because that’s where a lot of it shows up day to day, but it doesn’t stay contained there at all.\n\nLike, my mind will jump from worrying that our lab’s new partnership might mean my position gets cut, to worrying that my data are wrong or that I missed something obvious, and then suddenly I’m thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up if things go badly. Then it turns into worrying that my family thinks I’m falling behind in life, or that I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s like one worry hands off to the next one. I’ll start with something concrete, like work, but then it spreads into money, family, the future, whether I’m making the right choices… so no, I wouldn’t say it’s mostly one topic. It feels like it covers almost everything important.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think through your worries in a certain order or sequence to try to relieve your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say there’s a certain order, no. It’s not like I have to start with one worry and then move to the next in a specific sequence to feel okay.\n\nIt’s more disorganized than that. I’ll think I’m trying to “figure it out,” but my mind kind of jumps around depending on what feels most urgent or threatening in that moment. Sometimes I’ll start with one concern, then another one pops in before I’ve even finished thinking through the first one, and then I circle back again. But it doesn’t feel like a ritual or a set pattern that I have to complete.\n\nI guess I do sometimes feel like I need to keep thinking until I’ve covered everything, if that makes sense, but I haven’t noticed an actual sequence — like step one, step two, step three — that relieves the anxiety. If anything, trying to think it through usually just becomes this messy loop, and I don’t come out of it feeling much better.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries been interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. It’s gotten to where it’s not just something happening in my head anymore. It’s actually messing with what I can get done and how I show up for people.\n\nThe clearest example is that I missed an internal deadline at the lab. It wasn’t even some huge public presentation or anything, it was more like getting my writing and data review organized for our team, but I just couldn’t pull it together. I kept going back through the data, second-guessing how I was wording things, worrying that I’d missed something or that the report wouldn’t be clear enough, and then I’d get overwhelmed and sort of freeze. So instead of making progress, I’d spend all this time circling around it, and the deadline passed. That felt really bad, because I’m usually someone who tries hard to be reliable.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even routine things feel like too much lately. Preparing reports feels like this mountain, even when it should be straightforward. I’ve stopped being consistent with art nights, which used to be something I actually looked forward to, because by the time it comes around I feel too mentally drained or behind on everything else. And at home, even cooking for my family sometimes feels like another responsibility I can’t keep up with, even though it’s not supposed to be a big deal.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s significant. It’s affecting my work, my relationships, and just basic day-to-day responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific when you're worried to try to make the worrying stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t think there’s one specific thing I have to do, like a set behavior that makes it stop.\n\nI mean, when I’m worried, I do try to deal with it somehow — like I’ll think it through, or check whether I forgot something, or mentally go over what could go wrong — but it doesn’t feel like there’s a clear “if I do this, then the worry shuts off” kind of thing. It’s more like I’m trying to get relief, or certainty, I guess, but I don’t really get it.\n\nSometimes I’ll start doing something because I feel like I should be able to solve the worry, but then it just keeps expanding. So I don’t know if that counts as doing something specific. It doesn’t feel like a ritual exactly. It feels more like I’m stuck trying to reassure myself and not really succeeding.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, I would say it causes me a lot of distress. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in, because it feels like my mind just won’t stay in one place. It keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and each one feels serious in the moment.\n\nLike, I’ll be thinking about whether the lab’s new partnership could somehow mean my position gets cut, and before I can even really settle that thought, I’m worrying that my data are wrong or that I missed something important. Then it shifts to bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. It’s like there’s always another angle my brain finds to be scared about.\n\nAnd it’s not just mild concern. It feels upsetting and exhausting, because I can’t really tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough, move on.” Even when I know some of it might be hypothetical, it still feels like I have to keep thinking about it. So yes… it’s been causing a lot of distress.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular area in your life where your worries seem to originate or converge, or do they seem to be scattered across multiple domains?\n\nPatient: It feels scattered across a lot of domains, honestly. I wish I could say it all starts from one thing, because then maybe it would feel more manageable, but it doesn’t really work that way for me.\n\nLike I said, part of why I came in is that my mind is constantly jumping around. One minute I’m worrying about the lab’s new partnership and whether that could somehow mean my position gets cut, even if no one has actually said that. Then it shifts into whether my data are wrong, or whether I missed something important in what I’m working on. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school because I haven’t handled things perfectly.\n\nSo it’s not just work, although work is a big piece of it. It kind of spreads out from there into money, family, the future, school, whether I’m making the right choices… it all starts connecting in my head. It’s like one worry opens the door to three more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think about certain things in a specific, repetitive way to try to calm your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a specific repetitive way, at least. I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things over and over, but it doesn’t feel like I have a particular mental sequence I have to run through to calm down, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more messy than that. Like I’ll try to reason something out, or replay what I know, or imagine possible outcomes, but it’s not like I have to think a certain phrase or count things or go through the exact same steps in my head. It’s more like I’m searching for an answer that would make me feel okay, and then I don’t really find one, so I keep circling.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not a specific mental ritual. More just getting trapped in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: No… not most of the time. I mean, I try to. I really do. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just focus on this one thing,” like finishing a report or checking an assay, something concrete where there’s an obvious next step. But it doesn’t stay there.\n\nOne worry kind of sets off another, and then another, and before I know it I’m not really doing the task anymore. I’m just sitting there staring at the screen, but in my head I’m running through every possible way things could go wrong. Like if this result is off, then maybe I missed something, and if I missed something then maybe the whole report is wrong, and then what does that mean for my work, or my future, or… it just branches out really fast.\n\nAnd I know from the outside it probably sounds like I should just be able to say, “Stop, go back to what you were doing,” but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple. It feels like if I don’t keep thinking about it, I’m being careless somehow. So no, I wouldn’t say I can control it most of the time. It feels more like it takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is worse than what most people would experience in the same situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is worse than what most people would have in the same situations. I know everyone worries about work or money or their future, so I don’t want to make it sound like I think I’m the only person who has stress. But the way it happens for me feels… disproportionate, I guess.\n\nLike, I came in because my mind is constantly jumping from one thing to the next. It’ll start with something like our lab’s new partnership, and I’ll get stuck on whether that somehow means my position could get cut, even though I don’t actually have proof that’s happening. Then almost immediately I’m thinking, what if my data are wrong, what if I missed something, what if I look incompetent. And then it’s like my brain doesn’t stop there — it goes to bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo compared to other people, I feel like they might be concerned about one of those things and then move on, or at least keep it in proportion. For me it feels like everything becomes connected and urgent, even when nothing has actually happened yet. That’s the part that makes me feel like it’s more than normal worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it. I think at first I wanted to tell myself it was only affecting work, like it was just me being overwhelmed at the lab, but it’s definitely spilling over into other people’s lives too.\n\nLike, I missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn’t get myself organized enough to finish the writing and review the data properly. And then after that, everything felt heavier, if that makes sense. Even normal responsibilities started feeling like too much to keep up with — preparing reports, answering people clearly, doing basic things on time.\n\nAnd with friends and family… I’ve been flaking more. I’m supposed to show up for art nights with friends, and I’ll either cancel or be there but not really there mentally, because I’m still stuck thinking about what I didn’t finish or what I might have messed up. And at home, even something like cooking for my family has started to feel like this huge task. It’s not that I don’t care about them — I do — but I get so overwhelmed that I put things off or shut down, and then I feel guilty because I know they can tell.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting my relationships. Maybe not in some dramatic way where everyone is angry at me, but more like I’m becoming less reliable and less present, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Is there usually something that sets off your worrying, like a specific event or situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes there’s a specific thing, but honestly it doesn’t take much. It can start with something concrete, like hearing about our lab’s new partnership, and then my brain immediately jumps to, “Okay, does that mean they’re restructuring, does that mean my position could get cut?” Even if nobody has said that. It just becomes this whole chain.\n\nOr I’ll be working with data and notice one result that doesn’t look exactly how I expected, and then I’m thinking maybe my data are wrong, maybe I missed something obvious, maybe everyone’s going to realize I don’t know what I’m doing. And then somehow it doesn’t stay about the data. It turns into bills piling up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school… it’s like one small trigger opens up every other worry I have.\n\nSo yes, there are situations that set it off, especially anything around work or my future, but the problem is that once it starts, it spreads way beyond whatever actually happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… very much. That’s honestly a huge part of why I came in. It’s like my mind is almost always living a few steps ahead of where I actually am, but not in a useful planning way — more like it’s scanning for what could go wrong next.

So if something happens at work, even something that might be neutral, like our lab’s new partnership, I don’t just think, “Okay, that’s a change.” I start wondering if that means people are going to get moved around, or if my position could be cut, or if I’m not going to have a place there anymore. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that, I’m worrying about whether my data are wrong, whether I missed something important, whether someone’s going to look at my work and realize I messed everything up.

And then it spreads even further into things that haven’t happened. Like, I’ll start thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind in life, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school before I’ve even really had the chance to get there. It’s not just one future worry — it’s like one possible problem opens the door to five more, and they all feel urgent even though they’re still just “what ifs.”

So yes… it’s been causing a lot of distress.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular area in your life where your worries seem to originate or converge, or do they seem to be scattered across multiple domains?\n\nPatient: It feels scattered across a lot of domains, honestly. I wish I could say it all starts from one thing, because then maybe it would feel more manageable, but it doesn’t really work that way for me.\n\nLike I said, part of why I came in is that my mind is constantly jumping around. One minute I’m worrying about the lab’s new partnership and whether that could somehow mean my position gets cut, even if no one has actually said that. Then it shifts into whether my data are wrong, or whether I missed something important in what I’m working on. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school because I haven’t handled things perfectly.\n\nSo it’s not just work, although work is a big piece of it. It kind of spreads out from there into money, family, the future, school, whether I’m making the right choices… it all starts connecting in my head. It’s like one worry opens the door to three more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think about certain things in a specific, repetitive way to try to calm your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a specific repetitive way, at least. I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things over and over, but it doesn’t feel like I have a particular mental sequence I have to run through to calm down, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more messy than that. Like I’ll try to reason something out, or replay what I know, or imagine possible outcomes, but it’s not like I have to think a certain phrase or count things or go through the exact same steps in my head. It’s more like I’m searching for an answer that would make me feel okay, and then I don’t really find one, so I keep circling.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not a specific mental ritual. More just getting trapped in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: No… not most of the time. I mean, I try to. I really do. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just focus on this one thing,” like finishing a report or checking an assay, something concrete where there’s an obvious next step. But it doesn’t stay there.\n\nOne worry kind of sets off another, and then another, and before I know it I’m not really doing the task anymore. I’m just sitting there staring at the screen, but in my head I’m running through every possible way things could go wrong. Like if this result is off, then maybe I missed something, and if I missed something then maybe the whole report is wrong, and then what does that mean for my work, or my future, or… it just branches out really fast.\n\nAnd I know from the outside it probably sounds like I should just be able to say, “Stop, go back to what you were doing,” but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple. It feels like if I don’t keep thinking about it, I’m being careless somehow. So no, I wouldn’t say I can control it most of the time. It feels more like it takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is worse than what most people would experience in the same situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is worse than what most people would have in the same situations. I know everyone worries about work or money or their future, so I don’t want to make it sound like I think I’m the only person who has stress. But the way it happens for me feels… disproportionate, I guess.\n\nLike, I came in because my mind is constantly jumping from one thing to the next. It’ll start with something like our lab’s new partnership, and I’ll get stuck on whether that somehow means my position could get cut, even though I don’t actually have proof that’s happening. Then almost immediately I’m thinking, what if my data are wrong, what if I missed something, what if I look incompetent. And then it’s like my brain doesn’t stop there — it goes to bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo compared to other people, I feel like they might be concerned about one of those things and then move on, or at least keep it in proportion. For me it feels like everything becomes connected and urgent, even when nothing has actually happened yet. That’s the part that makes me feel like it’s more than normal worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it. I think at first I wanted to tell myself it was only affecting work, like it was just me being overwhelmed at the lab, but it’s definitely spilling over into other people’s lives too.\n\nLike, I missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn’t get myself organized enough to finish the writing and review the data properly. And then after that, everything felt heavier, if that makes sense. Even normal responsibilities started feeling like too much to keep up with — preparing reports, answering people clearly, doing basic things on time.\n\nAnd with friends and family… I’ve been flaking more. I’m supposed to show up for art nights with friends, and I’ll either cancel or be there but not really there mentally, because I’m still stuck thinking about what I didn’t finish or what I might have messed up. And at home, even something like cooking for my family has started to feel like this huge task. It’s not that I don’t care about them — I do — but I get so overwhelmed that I put things off or shut down, and then I feel guilty because I know they can tell.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting my relationships. Maybe not in some dramatic way where everyone is angry at me, but more like I’m becoming less reliable and less present, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Is there usually something that sets off your worrying, like a specific event or situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes there’s a specific thing, but honestly it doesn’t take much. It can start with something concrete, like hearing about our lab’s new partnership, and then my brain immediately jumps to, “Okay, does that mean they’re restructuring, does that mean my position could get cut?” Even if nobody has said that. It just becomes this whole chain.\n\nOr I’ll be working with data and notice one result that doesn’t look exactly how I expected, and then I’m thinking maybe my data are wrong, maybe I missed something obvious, maybe everyone’s going to realize I don’t know what I’m doing. And then somehow it doesn’t stay about the data. It turns into bills piling up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school… it’s like one small trigger opens up every other worry I have.\n\nSo yes, there are situations that set it off, especially anything around work or my future, but the problem is that once it starts, it spreads way beyond whatever actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. That’s honestly a huge part of why I came in. It’s like my mind is almost always living a few steps ahead of where I actually am, but not in a useful planning way — more like it’s scanning for what could go wrong next.\n\nSo if something happens at work, even something that might be neutral, like our lab’s new partnership, I don’t just think, “Okay, that’s a change.” I start wondering if that means people are going to get moved around, or if my position could be cut, or if I’m not going to have a place there anymore. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that, I’m worrying about whether my data are wrong, whether I missed something important, whether someone’s going to look at my work and realize I messed everything up.\n\nAnd then it spreads even further into things that haven’t happened. Like, I’ll start thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind in life, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school before I’ve even really had the chance to get there. It’s not just one future worry — it’s like one possible problem opens the door to five more, and they all feel urgent even though they’re still just “what ifs.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It definitely covers a lot of different areas. I wish it were just one thing, because then maybe I could sort of contain it, but it doesn’t stay in one lane at all.

That’s kind of why I came in, honestly — my mind is constantly jumping around. Like I’ll start with work, worrying that our lab’s new partnership might somehow lead to my position getting cut, even if no one has actually said that. And then almost immediately I’m thinking, “What if my data are wrong? What if I missed something?” And then it just keeps branching out from there.

Before I know it, I’m not just thinking about the lab anymore. I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. It feels like every area connects to every other area, so one worry turns into this whole pile of them.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because my worrying has gotten really out of control. It feels like my mind just won’t stay on one thing — it keeps jumping from one problem to another, and every one of them feels urgent.\n\nLike at work, I’m a chemist, and our lab has this new partnership, and I keep thinking, what if that means they’re going to restructure things or cut my position? Then it turns into money stuff, like bills piling up, and then my family — I worry they think I’m falling behind compared to where I should be. And then I start thinking I’ve probably ruined my chances at grad school already.\n\nIt’s just… constant. It’s like my brain keeps making a list of everything that could go wrong, and I can’t really shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them or think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. It’s not usually like one single sentence repeating word-for-word, but it’s like the same chain keeps restarting no matter how much I try to push it away.\n\nI’ll tell myself, okay, just focus on this one thing — finish the report, check the assay results, answer the email, whatever. But then one worry pops up, like maybe I made a mistake somewhere, and then that turns into, well, if I made a mistake then someone’s going to notice, and then maybe it affects the whole project, and then maybe it affects my job, and then I’m suddenly thinking about money or my future. And I realize I’ve just been staring at the screen for several minutes, not actually doing the task, because my head is running through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nIt feels really hard to interrupt once it starts. Like, I don’t have a specific thing I have to do a certain number of times, or a routine where I feel like if I don’t do it something bad will definitely happen.\n\nI mean, I do sometimes try to calm myself down by checking things or going back over what I did in my head, but it doesn’t feel like a ritual exactly. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself, and then it only helps for a second, if it helps at all. But I don’t think I have, like, specific behaviors that I’m compelled to do in a fixed way. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about certain things in a specific way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do, exactly. I mean… I definitely get stuck thinking, and I try to “solve” the worry in my head, but I don’t think there’s a specific way I have to think about something to make the anxiety go down.\n\nLike, I’ll try to reason through it — almost like arguing with myself — “Okay, what evidence do I have that I messed this up?” or “What’s the realistic chance this actually happens?” But it doesn’t feel like a required mental ritual, if that makes sense. It’s more like I’m trying anything to get my brain to quiet down, and sometimes I end up going in circles.\n\nI don’t have a particular phrase I repeat or a certain mental order I have to follow. It’s just… a lot of analyzing, replaying, trying to reassure myself, and then the reassurance doesn’t really stick for long. So I guess the answer is no, not in a specific way that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d describe it as overwhelming in that way, no. I mean, I definitely get an urge to keep thinking about the worry, like to keep checking it from different angles in my head, but it doesn’t feel like, “I have to repeat this action” or “I have to do this mental step again or something terrible will happen.”\n\nIt’s more like… I feel uneasy, and then I want relief, so I might go back over something again mentally, or I might feel pulled to reassure myself somehow. But it’s not a specific repeated act, and it’s not like I have a set mental thing I repeat until it feels “right.” It’s just hard to stop the worrying once it gets going.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not really. I don’t notice a strong urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to the worries, at least not in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, although I don’t always call it “avoidance” in the moment. I’ll tell myself I’m just postponing something until I’m in a better headspace, or that I need more time to do it properly, but then it kind of turns into me not doing it.\n\nThe biggest example is at work. I missed an internal lab deadline because I just couldn’t get myself organized enough to finish the writing and review the data. It wasn’t that I didn’t care — honestly, I cared too much, if anything — but every time I sat down to go through it, I’d get tangled up in whether the wording was right, whether I’d missed something in the data, whether someone would question how I presented it. So I kept circling back, trying to make it feel safe to submit, and then I lost time. After that, even opening the report started to feel like this huge thing.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. Routine responsibilities feel like too much lately. Preparing reports, even ones that should be straightforward, feels like this mountain. Outside of work, I’ve skipped art nights I usually like going to because I start worrying I’ll be too tired, or awkward, or behind on other things, and then I just don’t go. Even cooking for my family has started to feel hard to keep up with, like there are too many steps and too many ways to mess it up or disappoint people.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do avoid things — or I delay them until they become worse. It’s frustrating because these are normal parts of my life, not huge dramatic things, but they feel like more than I can manage sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific thoughts or mental images that you feel compelled to think about to try to relieve your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t think there are specific thoughts or images that I feel compelled to bring up on purpose to calm myself down.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck on certain worries, and sometimes a particular scenario will keep replaying in my mind, but it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing a specific image or thought because it relieves the anxiety. If anything, it usually makes me more anxious. Like I’ll try to think through it because I’m hoping I can settle it somehow, but there isn’t some exact mental picture or phrase where I think, “Okay, if I think this, I’ll feel better.”\n\nIt’s more vague and messy than that. I’ll go back over the concern, try to remember details, try to imagine how something might turn out, try to reassure myself that it’s probably fine… but it’s not like a specific thought I have to think. It’s just my mind sort of grabbing onto the worry and not letting go.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not in that specific way. I haven’t really noticed anything like a required mental image or thought that I use to relieve the anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life, like work, or do they cover a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just one area. Work is a big one, because that’s where a lot of it shows up day to day, but it doesn’t stay contained there at all.\n\nLike, my mind will jump from worrying that our lab’s new partnership might mean my position gets cut, to worrying that my data are wrong or that I missed something obvious, and then suddenly I’m thinking about bills and whether they’re going to pile up if things go badly. Then it turns into worrying that my family thinks I’m falling behind in life, or that I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school somehow.\n\nIt’s like one worry hands off to the next one. I’ll start with something concrete, like work, but then it spreads into money, family, the future, whether I’m making the right choices… so no, I wouldn’t say it’s mostly one topic. It feels like it covers almost everything important.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think through your worries in a certain order or sequence to try to relieve your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say there’s a certain order, no. It’s not like I have to start with one worry and then move to the next in a specific sequence to feel okay.\n\nIt’s more disorganized than that. I’ll think I’m trying to “figure it out,” but my mind kind of jumps around depending on what feels most urgent or threatening in that moment. Sometimes I’ll start with one concern, then another one pops in before I’ve even finished thinking through the first one, and then I circle back again. But it doesn’t feel like a ritual or a set pattern that I have to complete.\n\nI guess I do sometimes feel like I need to keep thinking until I’ve covered everything, if that makes sense, but I haven’t noticed an actual sequence — like step one, step two, step three — that relieves the anxiety. If anything, trying to think it through usually just becomes this messy loop, and I don’t come out of it feeling much better.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries been interfering with your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. It’s gotten to where it’s not just something happening in my head anymore. It’s actually messing with what I can get done and how I show up for people.\n\nThe clearest example is that I missed an internal deadline at the lab. It wasn’t even some huge public presentation or anything, it was more like getting my writing and data review organized for our team, but I just couldn’t pull it together. I kept going back through the data, second-guessing how I was wording things, worrying that I’d missed something or that the report wouldn’t be clear enough, and then I’d get overwhelmed and sort of freeze. So instead of making progress, I’d spend all this time circling around it, and the deadline passed. That felt really bad, because I’m usually someone who tries hard to be reliable.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even routine things feel like too much lately. Preparing reports feels like this mountain, even when it should be straightforward. I’ve stopped being consistent with art nights, which used to be something I actually looked forward to, because by the time it comes around I feel too mentally drained or behind on everything else. And at home, even cooking for my family sometimes feels like another responsibility I can’t keep up with, even though it’s not supposed to be a big deal.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s significant. It’s affecting my work, my relationships, and just basic day-to-day responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific when you're worried to try to make the worrying stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t think there’s one specific thing I have to do, like a set behavior that makes it stop.\n\nI mean, when I’m worried, I do try to deal with it somehow — like I’ll think it through, or check whether I forgot something, or mentally go over what could go wrong — but it doesn’t feel like there’s a clear “if I do this, then the worry shuts off” kind of thing. It’s more like I’m trying to get relief, or certainty, I guess, but I don’t really get it.\n\nSometimes I’ll start doing something because I feel like I should be able to solve the worry, but then it just keeps expanding. So I don’t know if that counts as doing something specific. It doesn’t feel like a ritual exactly. It feels more like I’m stuck trying to reassure myself and not really succeeding.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, I would say it causes me a lot of distress. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in, because it feels like my mind just won’t stay in one place. It keeps jumping from one thing to the next, and each one feels serious in the moment.\n\nLike, I’ll be thinking about whether the lab’s new partnership could somehow mean my position gets cut, and before I can even really settle that thought, I’m worrying that my data are wrong or that I missed something important. Then it shifts to bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. It’s like there’s always another angle my brain finds to be scared about.\n\nAnd it’s not just mild concern. It feels upsetting and exhausting, because I can’t really tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough, move on.” Even when I know some of it might be hypothetical, it still feels like I have to keep thinking about it. So yes… it’s been causing a lot of distress.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular area in your life where your worries seem to originate or converge, or do they seem to be scattered across multiple domains?\n\nPatient: It feels scattered across a lot of domains, honestly. I wish I could say it all starts from one thing, because then maybe it would feel more manageable, but it doesn’t really work that way for me.\n\nLike I said, part of why I came in is that my mind is constantly jumping around. One minute I’m worrying about the lab’s new partnership and whether that could somehow mean my position gets cut, even if no one has actually said that. Then it shifts into whether my data are wrong, or whether I missed something important in what I’m working on. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be at 24, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school because I haven’t handled things perfectly.\n\nSo it’s not just work, although work is a big piece of it. It kind of spreads out from there into money, family, the future, school, whether I’m making the right choices… it all starts connecting in my head. It’s like one worry opens the door to three more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think about certain things in a specific, repetitive way to try to calm your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a specific repetitive way, at least. I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things over and over, but it doesn’t feel like I have a particular mental sequence I have to run through to calm down, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more messy than that. Like I’ll try to reason something out, or replay what I know, or imagine possible outcomes, but it’s not like I have to think a certain phrase or count things or go through the exact same steps in my head. It’s more like I’m searching for an answer that would make me feel okay, and then I don’t really find one, so I keep circling.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not a specific mental ritual. More just getting trapped in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worrying most of the time?\n\nPatient: No… not most of the time. I mean, I try to. I really do. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just focus on this one thing,” like finishing a report or checking an assay, something concrete where there’s an obvious next step. But it doesn’t stay there.\n\nOne worry kind of sets off another, and then another, and before I know it I’m not really doing the task anymore. I’m just sitting there staring at the screen, but in my head I’m running through every possible way things could go wrong. Like if this result is off, then maybe I missed something, and if I missed something then maybe the whole report is wrong, and then what does that mean for my work, or my future, or… it just branches out really fast.\n\nAnd I know from the outside it probably sounds like I should just be able to say, “Stop, go back to what you were doing,” but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple. It feels like if I don’t keep thinking about it, I’m being careless somehow. So no, I wouldn’t say I can control it most of the time. It feels more like it takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is worse than what most people would experience in the same situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is worse than what most people would have in the same situations. I know everyone worries about work or money or their future, so I don’t want to make it sound like I think I’m the only person who has stress. But the way it happens for me feels… disproportionate, I guess.\n\nLike, I came in because my mind is constantly jumping from one thing to the next. It’ll start with something like our lab’s new partnership, and I’ll get stuck on whether that somehow means my position could get cut, even though I don’t actually have proof that’s happening. Then almost immediately I’m thinking, what if my data are wrong, what if I missed something, what if I look incompetent. And then it’s like my brain doesn’t stop there — it goes to bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school.\n\nSo compared to other people, I feel like they might be concerned about one of those things and then move on, or at least keep it in proportion. For me it feels like everything becomes connected and urgent, even when nothing has actually happened yet. That’s the part that makes me feel like it’s more than normal worrying.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it. I think at first I wanted to tell myself it was only affecting work, like it was just me being overwhelmed at the lab, but it’s definitely spilling over into other people’s lives too.\n\nLike, I missed an internal lab deadline recently because I just couldn’t get myself organized enough to finish the writing and review the data properly. And then after that, everything felt heavier, if that makes sense. Even normal responsibilities started feeling like too much to keep up with — preparing reports, answering people clearly, doing basic things on time.\n\nAnd with friends and family… I’ve been flaking more. I’m supposed to show up for art nights with friends, and I’ll either cancel or be there but not really there mentally, because I’m still stuck thinking about what I didn’t finish or what I might have messed up. And at home, even something like cooking for my family has started to feel like this huge task. It’s not that I don’t care about them — I do — but I get so overwhelmed that I put things off or shut down, and then I feel guilty because I know they can tell.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting my relationships. Maybe not in some dramatic way where everyone is angry at me, but more like I’m becoming less reliable and less present, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Is there usually something that sets off your worrying, like a specific event or situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes there’s a specific thing, but honestly it doesn’t take much. It can start with something concrete, like hearing about our lab’s new partnership, and then my brain immediately jumps to, “Okay, does that mean they’re restructuring, does that mean my position could get cut?” Even if nobody has said that. It just becomes this whole chain.\n\nOr I’ll be working with data and notice one result that doesn’t look exactly how I expected, and then I’m thinking maybe my data are wrong, maybe I missed something obvious, maybe everyone’s going to realize I don’t know what I’m doing. And then somehow it doesn’t stay about the data. It turns into bills piling up, whether my family thinks I’m falling behind, whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school… it’s like one small trigger opens up every other worry I have.\n\nSo yes, there are situations that set it off, especially anything around work or my future, but the problem is that once it starts, it spreads way beyond whatever actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. That’s honestly a huge part of why I came in. It’s like my mind is almost always living a few steps ahead of where I actually am, but not in a useful planning way — more like it’s scanning for what could go wrong next.\n\nSo if something happens at work, even something that might be neutral, like our lab’s new partnership, I don’t just think, “Okay, that’s a change.” I start wondering if that means people are going to get moved around, or if my position could be cut, or if I’m not going to have a place there anymore. And then before I’ve even finished thinking that, I’m worrying about whether my data are wrong, whether I missed something important, whether someone’s going to look at my work and realize I messed everything up.\n\nAnd then it spreads even further into things that haven’t happened. Like, I’ll start thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind in life, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school before I’ve even really had the chance to get there. It’s not just one future worry — it’s like one possible problem opens the door to five more, and they all feel urgent even though they’re still just “what ifs.”\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one main area of your life or does it cover many different areas?\n\nPatient: It definitely covers a lot of different areas. Like, I’ll start thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind in life, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school before I’ve even really had the chance to get there. It’s not just one future worry — it’s like one possible problem opens the door to five more, and they all feel urgent even though they’re still just “what ifs.”\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about one main area of your life or does it cover many different areas?\n\nPatient: It definitely covers a lot of different areas. I wish it were just one thing, because then maybe I could sort of contain it, but it doesn’t stay in one lane at all.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly — my mind is constantly jumping around. Like I’ll start with work, worrying that our lab’s new partnership might somehow lead to my position getting cut, even if no one has actually said that. And then almost immediately I’m thinking, “What if my data are wrong? What if I missed something?” And then it just keeps branching out from there.\n\nBefore I know it, I’m not just thinking about the lab anymore. I’m thinking about bills piling up, or whether my family thinks I’m falling behind compared to where I should be, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. It feels like every area connects to every other area, so one worry turns into this whole pile of them."
  }
